## User

hi, can you initialize a project here? The goal is to create a thin web client with an infinite scrolling canvas that the user can make box-diagrams on and connect them. Essentially a finite state machine. This FSM should be loaded from text files that are local on the computer defined in json. Each box should do something - run a function in one of the files in the SRC directory within the directory where we're storing the FSM structural files. It could be a function in a library (only public functions of course) like in C, or it could be a bash script or something. We want to be agnostic to what the user wants to do. We should also be able to create data files that include things like structs and such. For now, let's build for lua only functionality - storing tables in data files with json using dkjson, and doing all of the control flow with lua. The web application should simply be a UI to manipulate the local files, but we might later implement an HTTP server that lets a user control it remotely or something like that - open to discussion. We want simple control flow, with if/then/else, for loops, and anything else that seems reasonable. The user should be able to create multiple of these "maps" and they should be callable from one-another (but for now let's just keep them separate) which means they each will need their own output directory. What do you think?

## Assistant

Empty directory. Before I scaffold (`mkdir docs notes src libs assets issues` per your init protocol, then write `notes/vision`), I want to think through a couple of design questions with you, since the answers shape the whole structure.

My read: the natural split is **engine** (lua FSM interpreter that loads `nodes.json` + `edges.json` and dispatches each node to a `src/` lua function or shell command) vs. **editor** (thin web UI — vanilla JS canvas, no framework, talking to a tiny localhost lua HTTP server that just reads/writes the FSM json files). Main tradeoff: pure-static-file editor (open `index.html` directly, use File System Access API) is simpler but Chromium-only and clunky for shell-script invocation; a tiny lua server is more setup but lets the editor *also* run the FSM and stream output back, which seems closer to what you want.

A few things I'd want pinned down before writing the vision doc:

1. **What does a "box" actually contain at rest?** A reference like `{kind="lua", file="src/foo.lua", fn="do_thing"}` plus a typed input/output port list? Or freer — just a label and a hunk of inline lua?
2. **State** — does the FSM carry a single shared "context" table threaded through transitions (each node gets `ctx`, mutates it, returns next-edge label), or does each node have its own typed inputs/outputs wired explicitly along edges (more like a dataflow graph than a classical FSM)? These look similar but feel very different to use.
3. **Edges and conditionals** — is `if/then/else` a *property of the edge* (edge has a lua predicate, runtime picks the first true edge) or a *node kind* ("branch" node with two output ports)? The node-kind version draws cleaner but adds box types.
4. **Data files vs. functions** — you mentioned storing structs as json tables. Are these globals the FSM can read/write, per-map memory, or just immutable constants the nodes pull in?
5. **Per-map output directory** — what lands there? Just run logs / last-result snapshots, or is it a persistent KV store the map writes to across runs?

What's your instinct on those? Once I know, I'll write `notes/vision`, then derive `docs/` and `issues/phase-1-*` from it per your protocol.

## Assistant

Good answers — I think we're ~80% of the way to a vision doc. Let me unpack Q2 and react.

**Q2 explained.** Two extremes:

- *Shared context* — one big table `ctx` lives for the whole run. Every box receives `ctx`, mutates fields on it, returns only a transition label like `"ok"` or `"fail"`. Wiring between boxes is implicit: box B reads whatever box A wrote into `ctx`. Easy to add a new field, painful to see what flows where (it's all invisible in the diagram).
- *Dataflow / wired ports* — each box declares its input args and its return values as named ports. Wires literally connect output port of A to input port of B. A box can only see what's wired into its inputs. The diagram *is* the data flow — nothing hidden.

Your Q1 + Q3 answers already pick dataflow: structured input args parsed into the UI, tuple returns as multiple output ports, conditionals routing on those values. That's the wired-ports world. **The per-map data files (Q4) then play the role of the "shared context" for things that shouldn't be threaded through every wire** — e.g. the running LLM chat history, accumulated tool results, scratch memory. Boxes call `data.get("conversation")` / `data.set(...)` to read/write. Best of both: explicit wires for the values that matter at this step, named data store for state that persists across many steps.

**Q3 — where conditionals live.** Three options, in order of how I'd rank them for your case:

1. **Predicates on wires.** Each output port can have multiple outgoing wires; each wire carries a small structured condition (comparator dropdown: `== > < contains matches is-nil`, plus a literal or another port reference). Runtime walks wires from the port and follows the ones whose predicate is true. UI shows a little tag on the wire (`> 4`). No new box type. Matches your "if greater than 4, run this box next" almost literally.
2. **Branch box (dedicated kind).** A `branch` box takes one input, has N output ports each with a condition slot. Cleaner diagrams when there are many cases (switch-style), but it's a new kind and feels redundant with #1 for the common 2-way case.
3. **Both.** Wires support simple predicates; branch box exists for big switches. Probably where we end up eventually but I'd start with #1 only and add #2 if it earns its keep.

**Q4 reaction.** Per-map memory + a small `data` API exposed to box functions feels right. We can have two flavors of data file: *constants* (read-only, loaded at start) and *state* (read/write, persisted on shutdown or on explicit `save` from a box). Both are json tables on disk in the map's directory.

**Q5 reaction.** Your tradition fits cleanly: every map has `output/` with a `logs/` subdir that's a symlink into `/tmp/<map>-logs/`, while `output/data/` (or similar) holds the long-term json the user explicitly chose to persist. I'd add a `output/runs/` for last-run snapshot json — useful for debugging without bloating the long-term store.

**One more thing** the LLM-orchestration angle surfaces: a lot of LLM calls are *async / slow*. Two ways to handle:
- Synchronous for v1 — boxes just block; the engine runs single-threaded and you watch progress in the UI. Simplest. Plays nicely with later swapping in the 3d-rts thread pool when it's ready.
- Coroutine-based from day one — every box is a coroutine, the runtime can have several "in flight" and a node only fires when all its input ports are filled. This is the dataflow-graph way and it's how you'd want it once threading lands. More upfront design.

Given your "don't worry about those too much yet" — I'd start synchronous, but design the box-call boundary so each box is `function(inputs) -> outputs` with no global side effects beyond the `data` API. That makes the later coroutine/thread-pool swap a runtime change, not a rewrite.

So my proposed direction to lock in before writing `notes/vision`:

- Boxes = structured `{kind, ref, inputs[], outputs[]}` with multi-output for tuples; UI fully parses/edits these
- Wires = `from_port → to_port`, optional structured predicate on the wire
- Per-map `data/` = read/write json tables, accessed by boxes via a small `data` API
- Per-map `output/` with `logs → /tmp/...` symlink, `runs/` for last-run, `data/` for persisted state
- Engine v1 = synchronous lua; thin localhost lua HTTP server backs the web editor and also runs the FSM
- LLM orchestration is the north star use case — strings flowing through wires, history in `data/`

Two final yes/no questions and I'll start scaffolding:

**A.** OK with predicates-on-wires (option #1) for v1, defer branch-box?
**B.** OK with synchronous engine for v1, coroutine/thread-pool later when 3d-rts's pool is usable?

And one I genuinely don't know your answer to:

**C.** For the box "function reference" — should a box be allowed to invoke a function from a *library* file (`src/lib/strings.lua` → public function `trim`), a function from a *local* file (`src/my-thing.lua` → `do_thing`), AND a shell command, all from day one? Or start lua-only (both library + local) and add shell later? Shell adds an escaping/IO surface that's worth deferring if you're not sure you need it on day one.

Calling a function from a library file should be as simple as "src/strings.lua/trim()". The shell script is also easy because it'd just be "src/trim.sh". We need both. 7	## Current behavior
8	
9	The bootstrap from issue 101 runs entirely on the main thread. There is

12	## Intended behavior
13	
14	The program runs two OS threads as described in `docs/004-architecture.md`:
15	
16	- The main thread polls raylib input, pushes input events to a queue,
17	  reads the published snapshot, and renders.
18	- A simulation thread runs a fixed-rate tick loop that drains the queue,
19	  updates state (initially: a single moving placeholder marker), and
20	  publishes a fresh snapshot.
21	
22	A user runs the binary and sees a placeholder shape moving in a circle
23	on the screen, driven by the sim thread, while camera/input remain
24	responsive on the main thread regardless of sim load.
25	
26	## Suggested implementation steps
27	
28	1. Create `src/010-config.h` with `SIM_TICK_HZ`, `MAX_UNITS`,
29	   `MAX_PROJECTILES`, and the snapshot struct shape (forward-declared).
30	2. Create `src/110-snapshot.c` / `.h` with a double-buffered snapshot
31	   pair, a `pthread_mutex_t`, and `snapshot_publish()` /
32	   `snapshot_acquire()` primitives.
33	3. Create `src/040-input.c` / `.h` with a fixed-capacity ring queue of
34	   semantic input events plus its mutex. Events are not yet defined —
35	   start with one placeholder type.
36	4. Create `src/120-sim.c` with a `sim_thread_main()` function that:
37	   - Sleeps to maintain `SIM_TICK_HZ`.
38	   - Drains the input queue.
39	   - Updates a placeholder "marker" position in a circle.
40	   - Publishes a snapshot.
41	5. Update `src/001-main.c` to spawn the sim thread before entering the
42	   render loop and join it after the window closes.
43	6. Render the placeholder marker each frame from the snapshot, never
44	   from sim state directly.
45	
46	## Related documents
47	
48	- `docs/004-architecture.md` — full architectural picture.
49	
50	## Related libraries
51	
52	- `libs/900-task-pool.h` — an action-array task pool over pthreads
53	  exists in the project. **It is not used by this issue.** The two
54	  pthreads here are dedicated, long-lived threads with a clear data
55	  boundary; substituting a task pool would be a separate redesign
56	  (and a separate issue). The library is listed here only so future
57	  readers know it exists. See
58	  `issues/114-coroutine-pool-library.md` (file kept under its
59	  original name per the append-only rule) for the rationale.
60	
61	## Task pool integration
62	
63	The two pthreads from this issue are the *containers* the task
64	pool would eventually run inside, not callers of it. If/when the
65	sim thread adopts the pool, the sim becomes a pool of N+1 threads:
66	the original sim thread as the orchestrator (still owns game state
67	mutation order, still runs the merge step) plus the pool's worker
68	threads (run slice-batched parallel-for tasks at priority 1-2 plus
69	self-rescheduling per-entity tasks at varied priorities).
70	
71	The main thread does not need the pool — its work is render-rate
72	input polling and rendering, both of which are inherently single-
73	threaded. Each issue 107-119's "Task pool integration" section
74	documents which priority its work would run at if the pool is
75	adopted.
76

80	A useful sanity test: deliberately `usleep` for 100ms inside one sim tick
81	and confirm rendering does not stutter — only the marker pauses for a
82	tick. This confirms the decoupling holds.

3	## Status

5	**Superseded by issue 127.** Captured during the iter4 design

16	## Why frames, not microseconds
17	
18	Earlier conversation suggested microseconds. On reflection, frames
19	are the right unit:
20	
21	- Game cadence is frame-bound, not wall-time-bound. A task that
22	  needs to "run roughly every render tick" is naturally expressed
23	  in frames.
24	- Frame counters are monotonic, cheap to read, and don't require
25	  `clock_gettime` syscalls.
26	- The cycler's natural rhythm is already discrete; frames map onto
27	  it cleanly.
28	
29	## Current behavior
30	
31	There is no library notion of periodicity. A task that wants to
32	re-run periodically does so manually: at the end of its action
33	chain, it calls `pool_spawn` to create the next iteration.
34	
35	Self-rescheduling tasks must:
36	1. Decide their own next-iteration priority.
37	2. Track their own arrival timestamps if they care about latency.
38	3. Re-spawn with all the same actions and args each time.
39	
40	This works but is repetitive boilerplate at every periodic site.
41	
42	## Intended behavior
43	
44	The library accepts a per-task periodicity spec at spawn time:
45	
46	```c
47	typedef struct {
48	    int every_n_frames;     // target cadence: re-run every N frames.
49	    int max_slack_frames;   // if a run is more than this many frames late,
50	                            //   library promotes priority by one for the
51	                            //   next iteration (sticky until a run lands
52	                            //   on time again).
53	    int floor_priority;     // never auto-promote past this.
54	} task_periodic_spec_t;
55	
56	task_id_t pool_spawn_periodic(task_pool_t *pool,
57	                               const action_fn_t *actions,
58	                               void *const      *action_args,
59	                               int               n_actions,
60	                               int               initial_priority,
61	                               task_periodic_spec_t  spec);
62	```
63	
64	The library:
65	
66	1. Increments a frame counter on each frame boundary (driven by the
67	   game loop calling `pool_advance_frame(pool)`).
68	2. Tracks the last-completion frame number for each periodic task.
69	3. When a periodic task completes, schedules a re-spawn at frame
70	   `last_completion + every_n_frames`. The re-spawn happens by
71	   inserting a marker into a per-frame "due" list.
72	4. If the actual completion frame exceeds the target by more than
73	   `max_slack_frames`, bumps `initial_priority` by one for the
74	   next spawn (sticky promotion until the task starts landing on
75	   time again).
76	5. Auto-demote-on-block (already in iter4) still applies.
77	
78	## Suggested implementation steps
79	
80	1. Add `int frame` field to `task_pool_t`. Add
81	   `void pool_advance_frame(task_pool_t *pool)` — called by the
82	   game loop once per render tick.
83	2. Add `task_periodic_spec_t` typedef and `pool_spawn_periodic`
84	   public API.
85	3. Internally, periodic tasks have an extra "rescheduler" action
86	   appended to their actions array (similar in spirit to the
87	   iter3-era synthetic dep-check, but for re-spawning rather than
88	   waiting). The rescheduler computes the next spawn frame and
89	   priority based on this iteration's actual completion frame
90	   versus target.
91	4. The pool maintains a small "frame-ordered due list" — tasks
92	   waiting for their target frame to arrive. Each call to
93	   `pool_advance_frame` walks tasks whose target frame ≤ current,
94	   pushes them onto the ready queue at their current priority.
95	5. Update `tests/000-index.md` blind-spot list to remove
96	   "periodics" once tests cover this.
97	
98	## Tests
99	
100	- `tests/NN-task-pool-periodic-cadence.c` — periodic at cadence
101	  N; assert it runs ~`total_frames / N` times across a fixed
102	  number of frame advances.
103	- `tests/NN-task-pool-periodic-slack-promote.c` — periodic with
104	  small `max_slack_frames` under artificial load; assert priority
105	  rises after slack is exceeded.
106	
107	## Related
108	
109	- `114-coroutine-pool-library.md` — the deferred `promote_if_late`
110	  flag idea originated there. This issue subsumes it.
111	- `107-unit-movement-on-terrain.md` — likely the first periodic
112	  consumer (per-tick movement update). When 107 transitions onto
113	  the pool, its requirements will pin down details here.
114	
115	## Out of scope
116	
117	- Wall-time-based periodicity. If real-time deadlines matter
118	  (e.g., audio), a separate mechanism applies; this one is for
119	  game-tick cadence.
120	- Adaptive cadence (e.g., "run more often when there's load to
121	  spare"). Initial design is fixed-N; adaptive can come later.
122	
```

1	# 124 — Task pool: stable-index task storage (iter5)
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	**Exploratory — not committed work.** Captured during the iter4
6	design conversation (2026-04-28) as one possible refactor of task
7	storage. As of 2026-04-29 the user has explicitly pushed back on
8	pre-planning this: whether to actually do it should be decided
9	based on a concrete need, not as part of a multi-iteration roadmap.
10	
11	Possible drivers if it ever becomes real work:
12	- Cheap iteration over live tasks (registry walk + tombstone skip
13	  is replaced by a dense `for i in 0..n_live` loop).
14	- Removing the tombstone accumulation in the open-addressed hash.
15	
16	For now: not blocked, not committed, not next.
17	
18	## Why this exists separately
19	
20	The iter4 conversation went through several variants of "use indices
21	instead of pointers" — full slab allocator, chunked layout,
22	swap-with-last, and finally the variant captured here: stable
23	indices via a free list, with priority queues storing those indices
24	rather than task pointers. Iter4 does not depend on it; iter4 just
25	swaps the queue data structure to arrays-of-pointers and keeps the
26	pointer-based registry. This issue is the consistency follow-up.
27	
28	## Current behavior (post-iter4)
29	
30	- Tasks are individually `calloc`'d on `pool_spawn`.
31	- The registry is an open-addressed hash table:
32	  `task_t *table[REGISTRY_CAPACITY]` keyed on `id & MASK`.
33	- On free, the slot is marked with a `TOMBSTONE` sentinel so that
34	  later lookups for unrelated ids that collided into the same probe
35	  chain still find their target.
36	- Live tasks are not enumerable cheaply — iterating live tasks
37	  requires walking all 4096 registry slots and skipping NULL and
38	  TOMBSTONE entries.
39	- Priority queues hold `task_t *` pointers. Each task carries a
40	  `queue_position` int for O(1) splice via swap-with-last in its
41	  current priority's array.
42	
43	## Intended behavior
44	
45	- Tasks are still individually heap-allocated. The pool gains a
46	  dense pointer array `task_t **task_ptrs` with `n_tasks`
47	  (live count) and `cap_tasks` (allocated capacity).
48	- A free-list `int free_slots[]` tracks unused indices in
49	  `task_ptrs`. Allocate: pop a slot off the free list (or grow
50	  the array if empty), store the new task pointer there. Free:
51	  push the slot onto the free list, leave the array entry
52	  unused.
53	- **Slot indices are stable** for a task's entire lifetime. Other
54	  tasks being freed does not change my index.
55	- The registry maps `id → int slot_index` instead of `id → task_t *`.
56	  Lookup is `task_ptrs[registry[id]]` — two reads instead of one,
57	  but the indirection is cheap (the pointer array stays hot).
58	- Priority queues store `int slot_index` instead of `task_t *`.
59	  Promotion/demotion moves an integer between arrays.
60	- Live-task enumeration is `for (int i = 0; i < cap_tasks; i++) if (task_ptrs[i]) ...` — an order-of-magnitude faster than walking the registry hash.
61	
62	## Why stable, not swap-with-last
63	
64	Earlier in the design conversation, swap-with-last (compact the dense
65	list on every removal) was proposed. Stable-indices won out because:
66	
67	1. Priority queues hold slot indices. Swap-with-last in the dense
68	   list would invalidate every queue entry pointing at the moved
69	   slot, requiring fix-up walks.
70	2. The cost of holes in the dense list is small — capacity grows
71	   slowly and never shrinks; iteration with NULL skips is still
72	   far faster than the hash walk it replaces.
73	3. Stability removes a class of bugs ("I held a slot index across
74	   a critical section and something else got freed").
75	
76	## Suggested implementation steps
77	
78	1. Add to `task_pool_t`:
79	   - `task_t **task_ptrs;`
80	   - `int n_tasks_alloc;` (capacity of the array)
81	   - `int *free_slots; int n_free; int cap_free;`
82	2. Replace the registry's value type from `task_t *` to `int`.
83	   Update `registry_insert`, `registry_lookup`, `registry_remove`.
84	   The TOMBSTONE sentinel needs reworking — likely an int value
85	   like `-2` to distinguish from "empty" (`-1`) and live indices.
86	3. Replace `pool_spawn`'s `calloc` + registry insert with: malloc
87	   the task, allocate a slot from the free list (growing
88	   `task_ptrs` if needed), store the pointer, insert
89	   `(id → slot)` into the registry.
90	4. Replace `task_free` callsites with: free the heap struct, push
91	   the slot onto the free list, clear `task_ptrs[slot] = NULL`.
92	5. Convert priority queue arrays from `task_t **` to `int *`. The
93	   per-task `queue_position` field stays an int. Worker
94	   resolves: `int slot = queues[p][i]; task_t *t = task_ptrs[slot];`.
95	6. Add a helper `task_t *task_get(task_pool_t *pool, task_id_t id)`
96	   that wraps the two-step lookup. Most internal callers use this.
97	7. Migrate every internal `task_t *` local that came from a
98	   registry lookup to use `task_get`. The sites are
99	   self-contained (one file).
100	8. Verify iter4's tests still pass; the public API is unchanged.
101	
102	## What stays the same
103	
104	- The public API (`pool_spawn`, `pool_result_slot`, `pool_is_done`,
105	  `pool_ref`, `pool_unref`) — all still take `task_id_t`.
106	- Action signatures (`task_ctx_t` unchanged).
107	- The behavioral changes from iter4 (single priority field,
108	  demote-on-block, promote-blocker, result_filled).
109	
110	## Tests
111	
112	- `tests/NN-task-pool-stable-indices.c` — spawn task A at slot S,
113	  spawn many other tasks, free a bunch of them, assert A's slot
114	  is still S.
115	- Existing iter4 tests should pass without modification.
116	
117	## Out of scope
118	
119	- Shrinking the dense array. Pool grows to high-water mark;
120	  shrinking adds copying or fragmentation handling that isn't
121	  worth it for a game process bounded by gameplay.
122	- Migrating the public API to expose slot indices. Slots stay
123	  internal; users still see GUIDs.
124	
125	## Related
126	
127	- `114-coroutine-pool-library.md` — iter4's locked scope; this is
128	  the iter5 follow-up.
129

```
1	# 127 — Task pool: frame-ring scheduling
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	TODO. Next concrete piece of work after 107 lands. Direct successor
6	to issue 123 (which is now superseded — periodics are subsumed by
7	this design as "schedule into frame N+k").
8	
9	## Why this exists
10	
11	Issue 107's Shape B movement (per-unit self-rescheduling tasks at
12	priority 2) exposed a fundamental architectural problem with
13	self-rescheduling-at-max-rate: **timestamp-based motion is
14	mathematically correct under variable cadence, but cadence
15	variability is unbounded.** A worker thread can be off-CPU for
16	1-3 seconds when the system is contended; when it resumes, the
17	unit's next step has to cover that real-time delta in one frame.
18	The result is a visual snap from old position to new position,
19	with no smooth interpolation.
20	
21	This is not a small bug. It's the architecture announcing that
22	"poll as fast as possible, math will fix it" doesn't work when
23	the underlying scheduling is non-uniform. The fix is to bound
24	cadence variability — make `dt` approximately constant by
25	locking task execution to frame boundaries.
26	
27	## Snap reproduction (motivating example, captured 2026-04-29)
28	
29	- 6 units, press T, all get random targets via
30	  `units_set_target` → `pool_spawn` per unit.
31	- 5 units move smoothly toward their new targets.
32	- 1 unit stays visually stationary for 1-3 seconds, then snaps
33	  (instantly translates and rotates) to a new position.
34	- Fans run at full speed while units are moving.
35	
36	Root cause: in `move_advance`, `now = GetTime()` is captured at
37	the start of the action; the action does its arithmetic; at the
38	end, `u->last_update_t = now` writes that *start-of-action* `now`
39	value. If the OS preempts the worker between the capture and the
40	write (as happens occasionally on a contended system), real-time
41	advances during the preemption but the captured `now` does not.
42	The next iteration sees `dt = GetTime() - last_update_t` of 1-3
43	seconds, computes a correct-but-large step, and the unit teleports.
44	
45	No band-aid in this layer fixes the issue without losing physical
46	correctness:
47	- "Read GetTime() at end of action" → drops the preempted time;
48	  unit covers less total distance.
49	- "Cap dt at 100ms" → same thing, more aggressively.
50	
51	The only correct fix: bound how often the action runs. If
52	movement runs exactly once per frame and frame rate is bounded
53	(`SetTargetFPS(60)`), `dt ≈ 16.67ms ± small jitter`. No
54	accumulation, no snap, no lost distance.
55	
56	## Intended design
57	
58	A **ring of frame slots**, each slot itself containing the same
59	ten priority queues we have today. Concretely on `task_pool_t`:
60	
61	```c
62	#define FRAME_RING_SIZE 8        // tunable — how many frames of lookahead
63	struct task_pool {
64	    // ... existing fields except the queue arrays ...
65	    struct {
66	        struct task **queues[N_PRIORITIES + 1];
67	        int queue_lens[N_PRIORITIES + 1];
68	        int queue_caps[N_PRIORITIES + 1];
69	        int n_ready_total;
70	    } frames[FRAME_RING_SIZE];
71	    int current_frame;             // index into frames[]; the slot workers drain.
72	    int next_frame_offset;         // == 1 typically — "next frame" is current+1 mod size.
73	};
74	```
75	
76	Workers only pop from `frames[current_frame]`. New spawns always
77	land in `frames[(current_frame + offset) % FRAME_RING_SIZE]`,
78	where `offset >= 1` (the rule below).
79	
80	### Spawning rules
81	
82	- **`pool_spawn(...)`** — implicit "schedule for next frame."
83	  Lands in `frames[(current_frame + 1) % SIZE]`. Cannot land in
84	  the current frame; mid-frame work is what *this* frame's tasks
85	  scheduled *previously*, not what just-spawned tasks request.
86	- **`pool_spawn_in(N, ...)`** — schedule N frames from now. N=1
87	  is the default. N=0 is rejected (or interpreted as N=1; TBD).
88	  N=2 is "two frames out." N is *relative*, never an absolute
89	  frame counter — relative makes sense regardless of how many
90	  frames have run, doesn't require user to track a global counter.
91	- **`pool_spawn_in_current(...)`** — escape hatch for code that
92	  legitimately wants to land in the current frame (e.g., a task
93	  that needs to react to user input *this* frame, like a click
94	  handler). Used sparingly. Could be omitted entirely from the
95	  initial implementation if we don't need it.
96	
97	### Frame advance
98	
99	Main thread calls `pool_advance_frame(pool)` after the render
100	loop's `EndDrawing()` to mark "this frame is done; please move on."
101	
102	Two possible behaviors when the current slot still has unfinished
103	work:
104	
105	1. **Stall (recommended).** `pool_advance_frame` blocks until the
106	   current slot is fully drained (n_ready_total == 0 across all
107	   priorities AND no parked tasks waiting on tasks in this slot).
108	   Main thread keeps rendering — animations, particles, camera
109	   pan — but game-logic frame doesn't advance until this frame's
110	   logic is done. This is the model that makes lockstep multiplayer
111	   determinism possible: all clients agree on logic-frame N's
112	   complete state before advancing to N+1.
113	
114	2. Auto-promote leftover tasks to next frame. Simpler, but mixes
115	   frame budgets and breaks determinism. Rejected.
116	
117	Stall behavior is preferred. If the game routinely overflows its
118	frame budget, that's a profiling problem, not a scheduling one —
119	fix it by speeding up the offending tasks.
120	
121	### Multiplayer determinism (motivating future use case)
122	
123	Frame-locked task execution means: given the same input at the
124	start of frame N, all clients produce the same end-of-frame N
125	state. Clients can't have one client's logic "race ahead" because
126	its CPU is faster — every client waits for its own frame N tasks
127	to drain before advancing. Then they exchange inputs for frame
128	N+1. Standard lockstep model. The frame-ring design is the natural
129	fit; a polling pool would have to be retrofitted.
130	
131	## What stays the same
132	
133	- Public API for action contracts (`task_ctx_t`, `action_fn_t`,
134	  `ACT_*` enum values, `slot_status_t`).
135	- Parking on blocked-target (iter4.5 semantics). Parked tasks are
136	  not in any frame slot; they're attached to their blocker's
137	  waiters list. When woken, they get pushed to a frame's queue
138	  (current frame? next frame? — design decision pending).
139	- Result-slot semantics, refcounting, registry.
140	
141	## What changes
142	
143	- `pool_spawn` signature gains a "frame offset" or grows
144	  `pool_spawn_in(offset, ...)` as a sibling. Default is `+1`.
145	- `pool_advance_frame` is new public API.
146	- The "queue" inside `task_pool_t` becomes per-frame. Internal
147	  representation grows; public API barely changes.
148	- Self-rescheduling tasks (movement) call `pool_spawn_in(1, ...)`
149	  in their reschedule action. Each iteration runs once per frame.
150	- The cycler runs *within* a single frame's queues, not across
151	  frames. Same logic, just per-slot.
152	
153	## Suggested implementation steps
154	
155	1. Add `frames[FRAME_RING_SIZE]` to `task_pool_t`. Move the
156	   existing per-priority queue fields into the struct.
157	2. Add `current_frame` and the offset / advance functions.
158	3. Rewrite `queue_push` to accept a target frame (defaults to
159	   `current+1`). Workers' `ready_pop_locked` reads from
160	   `frames[current_frame]` only.
161	4. Add `pool_advance_frame(pool)` that stalls until current
162	   slot drains, then bumps `current_frame`.
163	5. Update `pool_spawn` signature (and / or add `pool_spawn_in`).
164	6. Update `040-game-pool` wrapper to expose the new API.
165	7. Update `001-main.c` to call `pool_advance_frame` in the
166	   per-frame loop.
167	8. Update `050-units.c` so movement reschedule uses
168	   `pool_spawn_in(1, ...)`.
169	9. Verify: snap is gone. Fans no longer run at full speed during
170	   movement (CPU drops to "6 tasks × 60Hz = 360 iterations/s"
171	   instead of "hundreds of thousands per second").
172	10. Update tests: rewrite test 005 (priority cycler) to advance
173	    frames; add a frame-ring-specific test
174	    (`tests/NN-task-pool-frame-bucket.c`).
175	
176	## Open design questions for implementation time
177	
178	- **Where do woken parked tasks land?** Current frame (run this
179	  frame) or next frame (deferred)? Probably next frame for
180	  consistency.
181	- **What's the right `FRAME_RING_SIZE`?** 8 is a guess. A periodic
182	  scheduling 5 frames out needs SIZE >= 5. Larger sizes cost
183	  memory (one set of queue arrays per slot). 16 might be safer
184	  default.
185	- **`pool_advance_frame` stall semantics under shutdown.** If
186	  the pool is shutting down with pending frame work, do we drain
187	  or leak? Per `pool_destroy`'s existing contract, leak.
188	- **Interaction with promote-on-blocked-target (iter4.5).**
189	  Promoting a task from priority 7 to 6 within the same frame
190	  works the same. Promotion *across* frames doesn't make sense
191	  — a task in next-frame's slot can't be promoted into this
192	  frame's queue (we'd race against the drain). Document.
193	
194	## Related
195	
196	- `123-task-pool-periodics.md` — superseded by this issue.
197	  Periodics ("run every N frames") become a one-line idiom under
198	  this design: a self-rescheduling task uses `pool_spawn_in(N,
199	  ...)` in its reschedule action.
200	- `124-task-pool-stable-indices.md` — independent, exploratory.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

```
1	# 114 — Coroutine Pool Library (M:N over pthreads)
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	TODO
6	
7	## Current behavior
8	
9	`libs/` is empty. The project's only described concurrency primitives
10	are the two dedicated pthreads from issue 102 (main + sim) and the two
11	mutexes that guard the input queue and snapshot pointer swap.
12	
13	There is no general-purpose mechanism for spawning many cooperative
14	tasks onto a small set of OS threads — every concurrent piece of work
15	would need its own bespoke thread, queue, and lifecycle.
16	
17	## Intended behavior
18	
19	`libs/` contains a small, self-contained C library that multiplexes
20	many coroutines onto a fixed pool of pthreads. A consumer can:
21	
22	- Create a pool of N worker pthreads.
23	- Spawn coroutines onto the pool. Each runs on its own ucontext stack.
24	- Cooperatively yield from inside a coroutine to release the worker.
25	- Join on a coroutine to wait for completion.
26	- Destroy the pool and reclaim resources.
27	
28	The library has no dependency on raylib, no dependency on game state,
29	and no global mutable state aside from the per-thread "currently-running
30	coroutine" pointer that the yield primitive needs.
31	
32	The library is **available** to the rest of the project but **not yet
33	adopted** by it. The two-thread architecture in `docs/004-architecture.md`
34	remains the design of record. See "Why this exists separately from 102"
35	below.
36	
37	## Suggested implementation steps
38	
39	1. Create `libs/900-coroutine-pool.h` declaring the public API:
40	   `cpool_create`, `cpool_destroy`, `cpool_spawn`, `co_yield`, `co_join`,
41	   plus the opaque `cpool_t` and `co_t` handle types.
42	2. Create `libs/900-coroutine-pool.c` implementing the worker loop,
43	   the FIFO ready queue (mutex + condvar), the ucontext-based stack
44	   switch, and the coroutine lifecycle (READY → RUNNING → DONE, with
45	   join handshake).
46	3. Create `libs/900-coroutine-pool.info.md` listing each external
47	   function with its inputs, outputs, and call-site constraints (e.g.
48	   `co_yield` is only valid from inside a coroutine running on a worker).
49	4. Add a `libs/` section to `docs/000-table-of-contents.md` so the new
50	   files are discoverable.
51	5. Add a "Related libraries" cross-reference to
52	   `issues/102-threading-model.md` pointing at this library, with a
53	   sentence explaining that adoption is a separate decision.
54	
55	## Mechanical description
56	
57	A worker pthread is a scheduler loop. Each iteration:
58	
59	1. Pop the next ready coroutine off the shared FIFO (block on a condvar
60	   if the queue is empty).
61	2. `swapcontext` from the worker's own scheduler context into the
62	   coroutine's ucontext. The coroutine now runs on its own stack on
63	   this OS thread.
64	3. The coroutine runs until it either calls `co_yield` (which
65	   `swapcontext`s back to the worker) or returns from its top-level
66	   function (which falls through `uc_link` back to the worker).
67	4. The worker inspects a `finished` flag set by the trampoline. If
68	   set, it takes the coroutine's lock, marks it `DONE`, and broadcasts
69	   to any joiner. If not set, it re-pushes the coroutine onto the
70	   ready queue.
71	
72	`co_yield` is implemented as a `swapcontext` from the current
73	coroutine's context back to a thread-local "scheduler context"
74	established by the worker before each `swapcontext` in. The
75	thread-local also names the currently-running coroutine, so
76	`co_yield` does not need its handle as an argument.
77	
78	The ready queue is a mutex-guarded singly-linked list with one
79	condvar. A condvar broadcast signals workers to re-check the queue.
80	`shutdown` is a flag plus a broadcast.
```

1	SoraMind — Vision
2	=================
3	
4	A camping cluster. Three small Alpine machines at .11, .12, and .13, wired
5	together on the same gigabit LAN, far from the internet, asked to do
6	something neat. They do not have to be fast. They have to be alive. The
7	point of this project is not to ship a product. The point is to design a
8	system, carefully and deliberately, that runs an LLM-flavored creature on
9	three pooled brains, and to enjoy the process of figuring out exactly how
10	every piece fits.
11	
12	SoraMind is the mind. It is the inference engine and the research track
13	that explores how a mind of this shape could be built differently. The
14	things the mind ends up powering — a persistent D&D world, an ambient
15	notepad, anything else — live in their own projects that consume SoraMind
16	as a substrate. The first such consumer is `narrative-engine`, in a
17	sibling directory.
18	
19	Why these three boxes?
20	----------------------
21	
22	Because they are portable, sip power, and make a fine companion in places
23	without coverage. The home desktop, the GPU box, the cloud — those have
24	their own roles, and Claude and Claude Code remain the tool of choice for
25	high-power focused work at home. The minis exist for a different
26	relationship with computation: ambient, meditative, willing to take an
27	hour to think about whether the bard would lie to the captain of the
28	guard. They are good at being slow about things that benefit from being
29	slow.
30	
31	The deeper goal
32	---------------
33	
34	This is a software-design project, not a product project. The plan is to
35	build the inference engine from first principles, in C and LuaJIT,
36	designed for symmetric three-way distribution from the very first line.
37	No power-of-two constraints inherited from upstream. No layers of someone
38	else's design we need to untangle and patch. Every design decision is one
39	we made, with a comment explaining why, in a place where future-us can
40	find it.
```

## Assistant

Good. Now I have the full context. Let me consolidate what's locked in before writing the vision:

**Design locked in:**

- **Box** = `{kind, ref, fn, inputs[], outputs[]}`. Two caller kinds: (a) **binary caller** — shell script or binary, run it, get stdout back as outputs; (b) **source caller** — `src/foo.lua/fn`, `src/foo.c/fn` — dispatched through a **language driver**. Built-in drivers: lua/luajit (interpret), bash (run), C (compile on modified, cache binary). User-defined: a shell script the user writes that takes `(file, fn, args...)` and returns results.
- **Branch box** = dedicated node kind. N named output ports + always an "else" port. Routing is string or numeric comparison against output value. "else" re-wires to upstream — the retry/randomize logic lives in whatever box feeds the branch, not in the branch box itself.
- **State**: dataflow wired ports. Per-map json with `constant: true/false` sections. Per-map `tmp/ → /tmp/<map>/` symlink for state + run snapshots. Persistent user data in `output/data/`. Data file access is a lua library (like any other lib), not a built-in API.
- **v1 sync**: every box call wraps in `task_fn(inputs) -> outputs` — same signature as the pool's action chain — so threading integration is a runtime swap, not a rewrite. effil-jit is available when we're ready.
- **Libraries at hand**: `dkjson.lua`, `luasocket` (for the HTTP server), `effil-jit` (later). Vanilla JS canvas for the web UI.
- **North star**: orchestrating SoraMind inference calls on the Alpine cluster — strings as atomic values, text passed between boxes, history in `/tmp/` and explicitly pulled in only when a box needs it.

## Assistant

On FSM execution — three options, ranked:

**1. Server spawns a separate runner process (recommended).** The HTTP server handles only file I/O (read/write box files) and process management. When the user hits "run", the server execs `luajit soramech-runner.lua <map-dir>` as a child process and streams its stdout back to the browser over SSE (server-sent events — a one-liner in luasocket, no WebSocket complexity). The runner is the engine: it loads the map, walks the graph, invokes drivers, threads outputs through wires. Runner crash ≠ server crash. Multiple maps run as separate processes in parallel naturally. The runner can also be invoked from the command line without the UI at all.

**2. Server runs FSM inline as a coroutine.** Simpler to set up but the FSM shares the server process. A runaway FSM blocks file saves. Harder to kill a specific run without affecting others.

**3. Fully detached runner, server watches a pipe.** Most decoupled but unnecessary complexity for v1.

**On the language driver question:** yes, the driver system is exactly what the runner uses for every box call — including lua and C. The runner has no hardcoded language knowledge. It reads `drivers.json` to find the shell command for each file extension. A built-in driver for `.lua` files would just be a shell script that calls `luajit -e "require('<file>')['\''<fn>'\''](<args>)"` or similar. A user's rust driver would be their own script. Built-ins are first-class drivers, same format, no special cases. The "built-in" part just means we ship a `drivers/` directory with pre-written driver scripts for lua, C, and bash — users can edit or override them exactly like their own.

**On box files:** confirmed — `boxes/<id>.json` is self-contained and lists both its incoming and outgoing connections. The runner validates both ends agree at load time. If they disagree (one box was edited without updating the other), it's a hard error, not a fallback.

can you tell me the difference between 1 and 3?

## Assistant

The difference is **who owns the runner process**.

In **option 1**, the server forks and execs the runner as its child process. The OS gives you a pipe to its stdout for free. The server knows the PID, can kill it, can wait for it to finish, and gets notified when it exits. Standard parent-child relationship.

In **option 3**, the runner is launched independently — not a child of the server. It could be started by the user from the terminal, a cron job, another process, anything. The server has no handle on it. The only shared channel is something both parties agree to watch: a file in `/tmp/`, a named pipe (FIFO), or a unix socket. Server polls or `inotify`s the file to know when new output arrives.

**Option 3 is useful when** you want the runner to outlive the server, survive a server restart, or be triggered by something other than the UI. It's also the pattern if the runner is a long-lived daemon rather than a one-shot process.

**For us, since output just goes to files anyway**, the distinction mostly collapses. Option 1 is cleaner for v1: server spawns runner as a child, knows when it's done or crashed, can report that status back to the UI ("run finished" / "run failed with exit code 1"). The runner writes its outputs to `output/data/` and its logs to `tmp/`. The server doesn't need to proxy anything — it just tracks process state.

Option 3 becomes relevant later if you want to run a map headlessly from a cronjob and still have the UI reflect its status. We can add that without changing the runner — just add a lock file or status json the runner writes to that the server can read on demand.

## Assistant

That's the cleanest architecture. Let me state it back clearly:

- **Server** = thin file CRUD proxy. Receives HTTP from the browser, reads/writes box files, edge refs, data files, drivers config. Nothing else. User starts it with `luajit server.lua <map-dir>` when they need remote editing. Completely ignorant of the runner.
- **Runner** = separate program. User invokes `luajit runner.lua <map-dir>` when they want to execute the FSM. Completely ignorant of the server. Writes output wherever the map defines. Could be a cronjob, a CLI call, anything.
- **Browser** = editor only. Talks to the server (or, for local use, could talk directly to the same server running on localhost). Never knows whether a runner is active.

The map directory is the contract. Server reads/writes it. Runner reads/executes it. They share nothing but the files.

This also means the runner doesn't need to be lua specifically — it just needs to read the map format. Later you could have a compiled C runner, a distributed runner across the Alpine cluster, whatever. The file format is the stable interface.

24	  - A validate-map.lua utility that checks a map directory for format

36	   non-zero if invalid.
37	4. Write scripts/create-map.sh — takes a map name and maps-root path,
38	   creates the directory structure, writes empty meta.json and drivers.json,
39	   creates the tmp/ symlink to /tmp/<map-name>/.
40	5. Create maps/hello/ as an example:
41	   - One box calling src/hello.lua / hello()
42	   - meta.json marking it as the entry box
43	   - A data file data/state.json with one mutable field
44	6. Write src/hello.lua with a public hello() function that reads a name
45	   from its input and returns a greeting string.
46	
47	## Related documents
48	
49	- docs/001-architecture.md — full map directory layout spec
50	- docs/003-driver-system.md — driver contract referenced by validate-map
51	
52	## Notes
53	
54	The tmp/ symlink must be created with an absolute path target. The
55	create-map utility must handle the case where /tmp/<map-name>/ does not
56	yet exist and create it.
57	
58	validate-map.lua is the source of truth for format correctness — any
59	time the architecture doc and the validator disagree, fix the validator
60	and update the doc.
61	
```

### Tool result

```
1	# SoraMech — Architecture
2	
3	## Three programs, one data format
4	
5	SoraMech is three independent programs that share a common on-disk format:
6	the map directory. None of the three knows about the others at runtime.
7	
8	```
9	  Browser (index.html)
10	       |  HTTP (file CRUD)
11	       v
12	  soramech-server.lua          [map directory]
13	                               maps/<name>/
14	                                 boxes/
15	                                 data/
16	                                 drivers.json
17	                                 meta.json
18	                                 tmp/ -> /tmp/<name>/
19	       ^
20	       | reads & writes
21	  soramech-runner.lua
22	```
23	
24	### soramech-server.lua
25	
26	Thin HTTP proxy. Receives JSON requests from the browser and translates
27	them into file reads and writes on the map directory. No logic beyond
28	path validation and basic consistency checks (e.g., reject a delete if
29	the box is still referenced by another box's connection list).
30	
31	Started with: `luajit soramech-server.lua <maps-root> [port]`
32	Default port: 7700
33	
34	Endpoints (all operate on map files):
35	  GET  /maps                        list maps
36	  GET  /maps/<name>/boxes           list box IDs
37	  GET  /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>      read box file
38	  PUT  /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>      write box file
39	  DELETE /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>    remove box (validates no dangling refs)
40	  GET  /maps/<name>/data/<name>     read data file
41	  PUT  /maps/<name>/data/<name>     write data file
42	  GET  /maps/<name>/drivers         read drivers.json
43	  PUT  /maps/<name>/drivers         write drivers.json
44	  GET  /maps/<name>/meta            read meta.json
45	
46	### soramech-runner.lua
47	
48	FSM execution engine. Loads a map directory, validates the graph, then
49	executes boxes in dependency order. Each box invocation is wrapped in a
50	`task_fn(inputs) -> outputs` call — matching the 3d-rts thread pool's
51	action chain signature for future threading compatibility.
52	
53	Started with: `luajit soramech-runner.lua <map-dir>`
54	
55	Execution steps:
56	  1. Read all box files from boxes/
57	  2. Validate: all connection endpoints exist, branch "else" handled
58	  3. Determine entry box from meta.json
59	  4. Execute: resolve inputs, invoke driver, collect outputs, fire wires
60	  5. On completion, write last-run snapshot to tmp/last-run.json
61	
62	### index.html
63	
64	Static HTML + vanilla JS. Infinite-scroll canvas. Box diagram editor.
65	Talks to soramech-server.lua for all file operations. No run button —
66	the user starts the runner separately from the terminal.
67	
68	## Map directory format
69	
70	```
71	maps/<name>/
72	  meta.json          — { name, description, entry_box_id }
73	  drivers.json       — { ".lua": "drivers/lua.sh", ".c": "drivers/c.sh", ... }
74	  boxes/
75	    <id>.json        — one file per box (see Box file format below)
76	  data/
77	    <name>.json      — lua table as JSON; sections have "constant": bool
78	  drivers/
79	    lua.sh           — built-in lua driver (shipped with soramech)
80	    c.sh             — built-in C driver
```

## Assistant

_Tools:_

- `Read: /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/docs/001-architecture.md`

### Tool result

```
80	    c.sh             — built-in C driver
81	    bash.sh          — built-in bash driver
82	  tmp/               — symlink to /tmp/<name>/
83	    last-run.json    — snapshot of last run (ephemeral)
84	    logs/            — run logs
85	    cache/           — compiled binaries (C driver cache)
86	```
87	
88	## Box file format
89	
90	```json
91	{
92	  "id": "unique-string",
93	  "label": "Human name",
94	  "kind": "call",
95	  "ref": "src/strings.lua",
96	  "fn": "trim",
97	  "inputs": [
98	    { "name": "text", "type": "string" }
99	  ],
100	  "outputs": [
101	    { "name": "result", "type": "string" }
102	  ],
103	  "connections": [
104	    {
105	      "from_output": "result",
106	      "to_box": "next-box-id",
107	      "to_input": "text"
108	    }
109	  ],
110	  "ui": { "x": 120, "y": 340 }
111	}
112	```
113	
114	For branch boxes (`"kind": "branch"`):
115	```json
116	{
117	  "id": "classify",
118	  "kind": "branch",
119	  "inputs": [{ "name": "value", "type": "string" }],
120	  "ports": [
121	    { "name": "rogue", "predicate": { "op": "eq", "value": "rogue" } },
122	    { "name": "wizard", "predicate": { "op": "eq", "value": "wizard" } },
123	    { "name": "else" }
124	  ],
125	  "connections": [...]
126	}
127	```
128	
129	Connections are written to BOTH endpoint box files. The runner validates
130	at load time that both ends agree. Disagreement is a hard error.
131	
132	## Language driver interface
133	
134	Drivers are shell scripts. The engine invokes them as:
135	
136	  <driver-script> <file-path> <fn-name> <arg-count> [<arg1> <arg2> ...]
137	
138	Outputs are returned as JSON on stdout. Exit code non-zero = box failure.
139	See docs/003-driver-system.md for the full driver contract.
140	
141	## Execution model (v1 — synchronous)
142	
143	The runner walks the graph depth-first from the entry box. Each box call:
144	
145	  1. Collect inputs: read wired output values from predecessor boxes
146	  2. Invoke driver: shell out to the appropriate driver script
147	  3. Collect outputs: parse JSON from driver stdout
148	  4. Store outputs: held in runner memory, keyed by box id + output name
149	  5. Fire connections: enqueue boxes whose input dependencies are now met
150	
151	Each step is wrapped in a `task_fn` boundary so the runner can be
152	threaded later by substituting the synchronous executor with one backed
153	by effil-jit or the custom thread pool from the 3d-rts project.
154	
155	## Data files
156	
157	Persistent data lives in data/<name>.json. Format:
158	
159	```json
160	{
161	  "constant": false,
162	  "fields": {
163	    "history": [],
164	    "config": { "constant": true, "value": { "model": "soramind-default" } }
165	  }
166	}
167	```
168	
169	Top-level `constant: true` makes the whole file read-only. Individual
170	fields can be flagged constant within an otherwise mutable file.
171	
172	Ephemeral state (scratch tables, logs, last-run snapshot) goes to tmp/,
173	which is a symlink to /tmp/<map-name>/. It survives the run but not a
174	reboot — intentionally.
175	
```

## Assistant

Issue 101 done. Moving straight to 102 — the language driver interface.

### Tool result

```
1	# 102 — Language driver interface
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	open
6	
7	## Current behavior
8	
9	No driver scripts exist. The runner has no mechanism to invoke functions
10	in any language. There is no defined contract for how a driver receives
11	arguments or returns values.
12	
13	## Intended behavior
14	
15	A drivers/ directory exists alongside soramech-runner.lua containing
16	three built-in driver scripts: lua.sh, c.sh, and bash.sh. Each follows
17	the same interface. The runner invokes any driver identically regardless
18	of language. A user can write a fourth driver for any language by copying
19	the pattern.
20	
21	Driver invocation contract:
22	  <driver-script> <file-path> <fn-name> <arg-count> [<arg> ...]
23	
24	All args are strings. Structured values are JSON strings. Driver writes
25	one JSON value (or JSON array for tuples) to stdout on success. Exits
26	non-zero on failure, error message to stderr.
27	
28	## Suggested implementation steps
29	
30	1. Write drivers/lua.sh — sources the target .lua file via luajit,
31	   calls the named function with decoded JSON args, encodes return value
32	   to JSON on stdout. Single-value and multi-value (tuple) returns both
33	   produce a JSON array.
34	2. Write drivers/c.sh — checks mtime of .c file against cached binary
35	   in tmp/cache/. Compiles if stale (using gcc or cc, flags configurable
36	   via env). Invokes the binary with args as argv. Binary must write JSON
37	   to stdout per the contract.
38	3. Write drivers/bash.sh — sources the .sh file, calls the named
39	   function with args as positional params. Function must echo JSON to
40	   stdout.
41	4. Write a small test map maps/driver-test/ with one box per driver type
42	   to verify each driver works end-to-end before the runner is built.
43	5. Document the driver contract in drivers/README (plain text, short).
44	
45	## Related documents
46	
47	- docs/003-driver-system.md — full driver system spec
48	- issues/101 — map scaffold (tmp/cache/ directory created there)
49	- issues/103 — runner uses these drivers once they exist
50	
51	## Notes
52	
53	For lua.sh: luajit must be on PATH. The script should fail with a clear
54	error if luajit is not found, not silently produce wrong output.
55	
56	For c.sh: the cache key for compiled binaries is the absolute path of the
57	source file. A sha1 or md5 of the path is sufficient; the binary is
58	stored as tmp/cache/<hash>. Stale check: stat both files, compare mtimes.
59	
60	For bash.sh: functions in a sourced file may have side effects and access
61	the filesystem. This is expected and correct — bash boxes are intentionally
62	less constrained than lua or C boxes.
63	
64	The "no special cases in the runner" constraint means the runner must not
65	have any if-elseif chain on file extension. All dispatch goes through
66	the driver table loaded from drivers.json.
67	
```

1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	# Lua/LuaJIT language driver for SoraMech.
3	# Loads a .lua module, calls the named function with JSON-decoded arguments,
4	# and writes the return value(s) as a JSON array to stdout.
5	# Contract: <script> <file-path> <fn-name> <arg-count> [<arg> ...]
6	
7	DIR="/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech"
8	
9	FILE_PATH="${1}"
10	FN_NAME="${2}"
11	ARG_COUNT="${3}"
12	shift 3
13	
14	if [ -z "${FILE_PATH}" ] || [ -z "${FN_NAME}" ]; then
15	    echo "lua.sh: missing file-path or fn-name" >&2
16	    exit 1
17	fi
18	
19	if ! command -v luajit > /dev/null 2>&1; then
20	    echo "lua.sh: luajit not found on PATH" >&2
21	    exit 1
22	fi
23	
24	if [ ! -f "${FILE_PATH}" ]; then
25	    echo "lua.sh: file not found: ${FILE_PATH}" >&2
26	    exit 1
27	fi
28	
29	# build a JSON array of the raw argument strings for passing into lua
30	ARGS_JSON="["
31	SEP=""
32	for ARG in "$@"; do
33	    ARGS_JSON="${ARGS_JSON}${SEP}${ARG}"
34	    SEP=","
35	done
36	ARGS_JSON="${ARGS_JSON}]"
37	
38	# run luajit with the driver shim inline; the shim loads the module,
39	# decodes arguments, calls the function, and encodes the result
40	luajit - "${FILE_PATH}" "${FN_NAME}" "${ARGS_JSON}" << 'LUASHIM'
41	local json = require("dkjson")
42	
43	-- read driver arguments
44	local file_path = arg[1]
45	local fn_name   = arg[2]
46	local args_raw  = arg[3]
47	
48	-- locate libs (DIR symlink is not available inside heredoc; use the file's dir)
49	local script_dir = file_path:match("^(.+)/[^/]+$") or "."
50	package.path = script_dir .. "/../../libs/?.lua;" ..
51	               "/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/libs/?.lua;" ..
52	               package.path
53	
54	-- decode args array
55	local args_table, _, jerr = json.decode(args_raw)
56	if not args_table then
57	    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: failed to decode args JSON: " .. tostring(jerr) .. "\n")
58	    os.exit(1)
59	end
60	
61	-- load the module
62	local ok, mod = pcall(dofile, file_path)
63	if not ok then
64	    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: error loading " .. file_path .. ": " .. tostring(mod) .. "\n")
65	    os.exit(1)
66	end
67	
68	-- resolve the function
69	local fn = mod[fn_name]
70	if type(fn) ~= "function" then
71	    io.stderr:write("lua.sh: no public function '" .. fn_name .. "' in " .. file_path .. "\n")
72	    os.exit(1)
73	end
74	
75	-- decode each arg from JSON to lua value; strings are passed as-is
76	local call_args = {}
77	for i, raw_arg in ipairs(args_table) do
78	    local val, _, aerr = json.decode(raw_arg)
79	    if aerr then
80	        -- not valid JSON — treat as a plain string
81	        call_args[i] = raw_arg
82	    else
83	        call_args[i] = val
84	    end
85	end
86	
87	-- call the function; collect all return values into a table
88	local results = table.pack(fn(table.unpack(call_args)))
89	
90	-- encode results as a JSON array (each return value is one element)
91	local encoded = {}
92	for i = 1, results.n do
93	    encoded[i] = json.encode(results[i])
94	end
95	print(json.encode(encoded))
96	LUASHIM
97	
```

## Assistant

Now issue 104 — the synchronous executor. Reading it first.

### Tool result

```
1	# 104 — Runner: synchronous executor (task boundary)
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	open
6	
7	## Blockers
8	
9	- 103 (graph must be loaded and validated before execution)
10	- 102 (drivers must exist to be invoked)
11	
12	## Current behavior
13	
14	No executor exists. There is no mechanism to walk the graph and invoke
15	boxes in dependency order.
16	
17	## Intended behavior
18	
19	soramech-runner.lua, after a successful load/validate, executes the map:
20	
21	  1. Start at entry box
22	  2. For each box ready to fire (all input ports satisfied):
23	     a. Collect input values from wired predecessor outputs
24	     b. Invoke driver: look up extension in driver table, call driver
25	        script with file + fn + args
26	     c. Parse JSON from driver stdout into output values
27	     d. Store output values keyed by (box_id, output_name)
28	     e. For each outgoing connection from this box:
29	        - If predicate present (branch box): evaluate predicate against
30	          the output value; if true, mark target box's input as satisfied
31	        - If no predicate: mark target box's input as satisfied
32	     f. Enqueue newly-satisfied boxes
33	  3. Continue until no boxes remain in the queue
34	  4. Write tmp/last-run.json with all box outputs and exit status
35	
36	Each box invocation is wrapped in a task_fn(inputs) -> outputs function.
37	This boundary is the future threading integration point. In v1 the task
38	function runs synchronously in the main coroutine; in a later phase the
39	same function signature is handed to the thread pool or effil-jit.
40	
41	Error handling: if a driver exits non-zero, the runner logs the box id,
42	the driver's stderr, and the exit code to tmp/logs/, then halts. No
43	fallback, no skip, no continue-on-error.
44	
45	## Suggested implementation steps
46	
47	1. Write src/004-executor.lua — public function:
48	   - execute(graph, map_dir) -> ok, err
49	   Internally uses a ready-queue (lua table as FIFO).
50	2. The task boundary: define a local run_task(box, inputs, driver_table)
51	   function that invokes the driver and returns {outputs, ok, err}.
52	   This function is the swap point for threading later.
53	3. Predicate evaluation: a small local function eval_predicate(pred, value)
54	   handles op: "eq", "lt", "gt", "lte", "gte", "contains", "matches".
55	   "matches" is a lua pattern match. No arbitrary lua eval — structured
56	   predicates only.
57	4. Write tmp/last-run.json on completion:
58	   { ok=bool, boxes={ [id]={ inputs={}, outputs={}, status="ok"|"error" } } }
59	5. Write tests/002-executor-test.lua — runs maps/hello/ end-to-end, asserts
60	   expected output values in last-run.json.
61	
62	## Related documents
63	
64	- docs/001-architecture.md — execution model section
65	- docs/003-driver-system.md — driver invocation contract
66	- issues/103 — graph loader (graph argument comes from here)
67	- issues/108 — branch box predicate evaluation expanded
68	
69	## Notes
70	
71	The ready-queue approach (enqueue boxes as their inputs are satisfied)
72	is the natural path to concurrent execution later: in a threaded executor,
73	the enqueue step becomes a pool_spawn call instead of a direct function
74	call. The box output store (keyed by box_id + output_name) becomes shared
75	memory guarded by the pool's result-slot mechanism.
76	
77	Do not use os.execute or io.popen with complex shell pipelines for driver
78	invocation. Build the command as a table of arguments, invoke via
79	io.popen with a single quoted command string. If a more robust approach
80	is needed, write a small helper that builds the invocation string safely.
81	
```

```
1	# 105 — HTTP server: file CRUD API
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	open
6	
7	## Blockers
8	
9	- 101 (map format must be defined; server validates against schema)
10	
11	## Current behavior
12	
13	No server exists. Map files can only be edited by hand on the machine
14	where they live.
15	
16	## Intended behavior
17	
18	soramech-server.lua is a minimal HTTP server backed by luasocket. It
19	exposes a REST API that the browser uses to read and write map files.
20	It does nothing else. No runner management, no process spawning, no
21	streaming.
22	
23	Started with: `luajit soramech-server.lua <maps-root> [port]`
24	Default port: 7700
25	
26	Endpoints:
27	  GET  /maps                          — list map names (array of strings)
28	  GET  /maps/<name>/boxes             — list box IDs in this map
29	  GET  /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>        — read one box file (JSON)
30	  PUT  /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>        — write one box file (validates schema)
31	  DELETE /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>      — delete box (hard error if referenced)
32	  GET  /maps/<name>/data/<filename>   — read a data file
33	  PUT  /maps/<name>/data/<filename>   — write a data file
34	  GET  /maps/<name>/drivers           — read drivers.json
35	  PUT  /maps/<name>/drivers           — write drivers.json
36	  GET  /maps/<name>/meta              — read meta.json
37	  PUT  /maps/<name>/meta              — write meta.json
38	
39	All request and response bodies are JSON. Content-Type: application/json.
40	CORS headers must be set (Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *) so index.html
41	served from a file:// URL or a different origin can reach the server.
42	
43	Error responses: { "error": "message" } with appropriate HTTP status.
44	400 for schema violations, 404 for missing resources, 409 for constraint
45	violations (delete of a referenced box), 500 for filesystem errors.
46	
47	## Suggested implementation steps
48	
49	1. Write src/005-http-server.lua — top-level server loop using luasocket's
50	   tcp server primitives. Accept connections, parse HTTP request line and
51	   headers, dispatch to handler table by method + path pattern.
52	2. Route table: a dispatch table mapping {method, pattern} -> handler_fn.
53	   Pattern matching is simple prefix matching on the path segments —
54	   no regex needed.
55	3. Each handler receives {method, path_parts, body_string} and returns
56	   {status, body_string}. The server wraps these in HTTP framing.
57	4. Validate box writes against src/001-schema.lua before writing to disk.
58	   Reject with 400 and a descriptive error if validation fails.
59	5. For DELETE /boxes/<id>: scan all other box files for references to the
60	   deleted id in their connections list. Hard error with 409 if found;
61	   list all referencing boxes in the error body.
62	6. Write a simple smoke test: scripts/test-server.sh — starts the server,
63	   uses curl to create a box, read it back, update it, delete it. Asserts
64	   expected responses.
65	
66	## Related documents
67	
68	- docs/001-architecture.md — endpoint list and error format
69	- issues/101 — schema validator used in step 4
70	- issues/106 — browser client that calls these endpoints
71	
72	## Notes
73	
74	luasocket's HTTP server support is low-level. The server loop must handle
75	keep-alive connections correctly, or close after each response. Closing
76	after each response (HTTP/1.0 style) is simpler and sufficient for the
77	browser editor's access pattern.
78	
79	The server should log each request to stdout in a single line:
80	  [timestamp] METHOD /path -> status (ms)
81	This makes debugging easy without needing a log file.
82	
83	The server must not follow symlinks outside the maps-root. Validate that
84	every resolved file path begins with maps-root before reading or writing.
85	This is the only security boundary needed since the server is local-only.
86	
```

1	# 107 — Web editor: wiring and connection UI
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	open
6	
7	## Blockers
8	
9	- 106 (canvas and box rendering must exist before wires can be drawn)
10	
11	## Current behavior
12	
13	Boxes can be drawn on the canvas but there is no way to connect them.
14	Connection data exists in box files but is not visualized.
15	
16	## Intended behavior
17	
18	The canvas renders wires between connected boxes. The user can draw new
19	wires by dragging from an output port dot to an input port dot. Wires
20	can be deleted by clicking them and pressing delete.
21	
22	When a wire is created:
23	  1. Both endpoint box files are updated via PUT (connection added to both)
24	  2. If the connection creates a cycle that does not pass through a branch
25	     box, the editor warns but does not prevent the connection (the runner
26	     will reject it at load time; the editor is not the enforcement point)
27	
28	When a wire is deleted:
29	  1. Both endpoint box files are updated via PUT (connection removed from both)
30	
31	Wire rendering:
32	  - Bezier curve from output port dot to input port dot
33	  - Control points offset horizontally by ~80 world units to give a smooth S curve
34	  - Color: neutral (grey) by default; selected wire highlighted
35	  - Branch box ports: each port has its own wire color (cycling palette)
36	    so multi-port wires are visually distinct
37	
38	## Suggested implementation steps
39	
40	1. Write assets/js/006-wires.js — wire rendering (bezier) and hit testing
41	   (sample points along curve, check distance to cursor).
42	2. Drag-to-wire: on mousedown on a port dot, enter "drawing" state. Render
43	   a bezier from the source port to the current cursor position. On mouseup
44	   over a target port dot, create the connection. On mouseup elsewhere,
45	   cancel.
46	3. Type compatibility warning: if the source output type and the target
47	   input type are declared and do not match, show a warning tooltip on the
48	   wire. Do not prevent the connection — type declarations are hints, not
49	   enforcement in v1.
50	4. Connection persistence: after creating or deleting a wire, fetch both
51	   affected box files, update the connections arrays, and PUT both. The
52	   fetch-then-update pattern ensures we have the latest file state before
53	   modifying it (avoids overwriting concurrent edits from another browser).
54	5. Write assets/js/007-delete.js — delete key handler. If a wire is
55	   selected, removes it. If a box is selected, removes the box (prompts
56	   if the box has connections — lists them).
57	
58	## Related documents
59	
60	- docs/001-architecture.md — connection format (both-ends invariant)
61	- issues/106 — canvas and port dot rendering
62	- issues/108 — branch box port colors and predicate display on wires
63	
64	## Notes
65	
66	Wire hit testing on a bezier curve is non-trivial. A practical approach:
67	sample 20 evenly spaced points along the curve, find the minimum distance
68	to the cursor. If under a threshold (e.g., 6px screen space), the wire
69	is hit. This is fast enough for the number of wires expected.
70	
71	The fetch-before-PUT pattern in step 4 is important. Without it, a
72	connection added from two different browsers simultaneously can cause one
73	to overwrite the other's change. In v1, last-write-wins is acceptable
74	(no locking), but fetching first reduces the window.
75	
```

1	# 106 — Web editor: canvas and box rendering
2	
3	## Status
4	
5	open
6	
7	## Blockers
8	
9	- 105 (server must be running for the editor to load map files)
10	
11	## Current behavior
12	
13	No web editor exists. Map files can only be viewed and edited as raw JSON.
14	
15	## Intended behavior
16	
17	index.html is a single-file web application (no build step, no framework,
18	no dependencies beyond the fetch API and the Canvas 2D API). When opened
19	in a browser and pointed at a running soramech-server, it:
20	
21	  - Renders an infinite-scroll canvas with pan (drag) and zoom (scroll)
22	  - Loads all boxes for the current map and draws each as a rectangle
23	  - Each box displays: label, kind badge, input port list on the left,
24	    output port list on the right
25	  - Clicking a box opens an inspector panel on the right side showing all
26	    editable fields (label, ref, fn, inputs, outputs)
27	  - Editing any field in the inspector sends a PUT to the server immediately
28	    (autosave — no explicit save button)
29	  - Double-clicking the canvas background creates a new box at that position
30	  - Boxes are draggable; dragging updates the ui.x/ui.y fields in the box
31	    file via PUT
32	
33	The canvas coordinate system uses a camera transform (pan offset + zoom
34	scale) applied before drawing. All box positions are in "world" coordinates
35	stored in the box file; the camera transform maps world to screen.
36	
37	## Suggested implementation steps
38	
39	1. Write assets/index.html with a <canvas> element full-viewport and a
40	   sidebar div for the inspector panel.
41	2. Write assets/js/001-canvas.js — camera state (pan x/y, zoom), input
42	   handlers for drag-to-pan and scroll-to-zoom, a render loop using
43	   requestAnimationFrame, coordinate conversion functions world_to_screen
44	   and screen_to_world.
45	3. Write assets/js/002-boxes.js — box rendering (rectangle, label,
46	   kind badge, port dots on left/right edges), hit testing for click and
47	   drag.
48	4. Write assets/js/003-api.js — thin wrapper around fetch for each server
49	   endpoint. Returns parsed JSON or throws with the server error message.
50	5. Write assets/js/004-inspector.js — sidebar panel that populates a form
51	   from the selected box's fields and fires PUT on each input change.
52	6. Write assets/js/005-app.js — top-level: on load, ask the user for the
53	   server URL and map name, load all boxes, enter the render loop.
54	7. All JS files are loaded with <script> tags in index.html in numbered
55	   order. No module bundler.
56	
57	## UI design notes
58	
59	Port dots: input ports are drawn on the left edge of the box, one dot per
60	input, evenly spaced. Output ports on the right edge. Port dot size should
61	be large enough to click (at minimum 8px radius in screen space). Port
62	labels appear on hover.
63	
64	Kind badge: a small colored label in the top-right corner of the box.
65	  "call" → blue, "branch" → orange, "data" → green.
66	
67	Box size: calculated from the number of ports. Minimum height ensures the
68	label and badge fit. Width is fixed (e.g., 180px world units).
69	
70	Inspector panel: slides in from the right when a box is selected. Fields
71	rendered as labeled inputs. Input/output port lists are rendered as
72	add/remove lists (click + to add a port, click x to remove). Predicate
73	fields on branch box ports are shown as a comparator dropdown + value input.
74	
75	## Related documents
76	
77	- docs/001-architecture.md — box file format (ui.x, ui.y fields)
78	- issues/105 — server endpoints used by api.js
79	- issues/107 — wire drawing added on top of this canvas
80	
81	## Notes
82	
83	The canvas render loop should only redraw when state changes (box moved,
84	box selected, camera moved) — not every frame unconditionally. A dirty
85	flag is sufficient for v1; no need for a scene graph.
86	
87	The server URL and map name should be stored in localStorage so the user
88	doesn't have to re-enter them on reload.
89	
```

349	    if m == "OPTIONS" then return handle_options(client) end
350	
351	    -- GET /maps
352	    if m == "GET" and #p == 1 and p[1] == "maps" then
353	        return handle_list_maps(client, req, maps_root)
354	    end
355	
356	    -- GET /maps/<name>/boxes
357	    if m == "GET" and #p == 3 and p[1] == "maps" and p[3] == "boxes" then
358	        return handle_list_boxes(client, req, maps_root)
359	    end
360	
361	    -- GET/PUT/DELETE /maps/<name>/boxes/<id>
362	    if #p == 4 and p[1] == "maps" and p[3] == "boxes" then
363	        if m == "GET"    then return handle_get_box(client, req, maps_root) end
364	        if m == "PUT"    then return handle_put_box(client, req, maps_root) end
365	        if m == "DELETE" then return handle_delete_box(client, req, maps_root) end
366	    end
367	
368	    -- GET/PUT /maps/<name>/data/<filename>
369	    if #p == 4 and p[1] == "maps" and p[3] == "data" then
370	        if m == "GET" then return handle_get_data(client, req, maps_root) end
371	        if m == "PUT" then return handle_put_data(client, req, maps_root) end
372	    end
373	
374	    -- GET/PUT /maps/<name>/drivers
375	    if #p == 3 and p[1] == "maps" and p[3] == "drivers" then
376	        if m == "GET" then return handle_get_simple(client, req, maps_root, "drivers.json") end
377	        if m == "PUT" then return handle_put_simple(client, req, maps_root, "drivers.json", schema.validate_drivers) end
378	    end
379	
380	    -- GET/PUT /maps/<name>/meta
381	    if #p == 3 and p[1] == "maps" and p[3] == "meta" then
382	        if m == "GET" then return handle_get_simple(client, req, maps_root, "meta.json") end
383	        if m == "PUT" then return handle_put_simple(client, req, maps_root, "meta.json", schema.validate_meta) end
384	    end
385	
386	    json_err(client, "404 Not Found", "no route for " .. m .. " " .. req.path)
387	end
388	-- }}}
389	
390	-- {{{ serve
391	function M.serve(maps_root, port)
392	    local server, err = socket.bind("*", port)
393	    if not server then
394	        io.stderr:write("server: cannot bind to port " .. port .. ": " .. tostring(err) .. "\n")
395	        os.exit(1)
396	    end
397	    server:settimeout(1)  -- 1s accept timeout so we can handle signals cleanly
398	
399	    print("soramech-server listening on :" .. port .. " (maps: " .. maps_root .. ")")
400	
401	    while true do
402	        local client, cerr = server:accept()
403	        if client then
404	            client:settimeout(5)
405	            local t_start = socket.gettime()
406	
407	            local req, rerr = parse_request(client)
408	            if req then
409	                local ok_disp, derr = pcall(dispatch, client, req, maps_root)
410	                if not ok_disp then
411	                    pcall(json_err, client, "500 Internal Server Error", tostring(derr))
412	                end
413	                local elapsed = math.floor((socket.gettime() - t_start) * 1000)
414	                print(string.format("[%.0f] %s %s (%dms)",
415	                    os.time(), req.method, req.path, elapsed))
416	            else
417	                pcall(json_err, client, "400 Bad Request", tostring(rerr))
418	            end
419	
```
